### 1.2 Related works

In this section we will discuss theoretical works that are mostly
related to ours.

##### Two-player Matrix Games.

Two-player zero-sum matrix games have been studied extensively, from the
foundational work of ([Shapley, 1953](#bib.bib13)) to more recent
analyses of convergence in the unregularized setting ([Mertikopoulos et
al., 2018](#bib.bib14); [Daskalakis and Panageas, 2018](#bib.bib15);
[Wei et al., 2021](#bib.bib16)). In settings with KL regularization,
faster last-iterate linear convergence guarantees have also been
established ([Cen et al., 2023](#bib.bib25); [Cen et al.,
2024](#bib.bib12)). However, these works focus on the tabular
full-information setting. Closer to our setting are [O’Donoghue et al.
(2021)](#bib.bib8); [Yang et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7), where the payoff
matrix is unknown and must be estimated through noisy oracle queries.
[O’Donoghue et al. (2021)](#bib.bib8) introduced UCB/optimism ([Lai,
1987](#bib.bib18)) and K-Learning (similar to Thompson sampling ([Russo
et al., 2018](#bib.bib19))) based approaches in the tabular
unregularized setting, while [Yang et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) proposed a
value-incentivized approach ([Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib3)) and
established regret guarantees in the regularized setting with function
approximation. Learning from preference feedback has also been studied
in [Ye et al. (2024)](#bib.bib17). However, none of these approaches
exploit the structure of the KL-regularized problem to achieve
logarithmic regret; instead, they maintain $`\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})`$
regret.

##### Two-player Markov Games.

Two-player zero-sum Markov games ([Littman, 1994](#bib.bib20))
generalize single-agent MDPs to competitive two-player settings. The
problem has widely studied in the finite horizon tabular setting ([Bai
and Jin, 2020](#bib.bib22); [Bai et al., 2020](#bib.bib21); [Liu et al.,
2021](#bib.bib23)), under linear function approximation ([Xie et al.,
2023](#bib.bib11); [Chen et al., 2022](#bib.bib9)), in the context of
general function approximation ([Jin et al., 2022](#bib.bib24); [Huang
et al., 2022](#bib.bib38)) and under the infinite-horizon setting
([Sidford et al., 2020](#bib.bib42); [Sayin et al., 2021](#bib.bib41)).
Many of these algorithms use optimism-based methods, using upper and
lower bounds on the value functions to define a general-sum game. They
sidestep the need to solve for a NE in general-sum games by employing
Coarse Correlated Equilibrium (CCE)-based sampling, exploiting the fact
that in two-player settings the dual gap of a joint policy over the
joint action space matches that of the corresponding marginal
independent policies. In addition, there have also been works solving
the problem under full information setting with exact/first order oracle
access ([Zeng et al., 2022](#bib.bib40); [Cen et al., 2023](#bib.bib25);
[Cen et al., 2024](#bib.bib12); [Yang and Ma, 2023](#bib.bib26)) and
offline setting ([Cui and Du, 2022](#bib.bib27); [Zhong et al.,
2022](#bib.bib29); [Yan et al., 2024](#bib.bib30)). All prior works
consider the unregularized setting, except [Zeng et al.
(2022)](#bib.bib40); [Cen et al. (2024)](#bib.bib12), which achieves
linear convergence under entropy regularization, compared to the
$`\mathcal{O}(T^{-1})`$ rate in the unregularized case.

##### Entropy/KL Regularization in Decision Making.

Entropy regularization methods are widely used as a mechanism for
encouraging exploration ([Neu et al., 2017](#bib.bib43); [Geist et al.,
2019](#bib.bib44)). These methods have been studied from a policy
optimization perspective with some form of gradient oracle/first-order
oracle access in single agent RL ([Cen et al., 2022b](#bib.bib45); [Lan,
2023](#bib.bib46)), zero-sum matrix and Markov games ([Cen et al.,
2023](#bib.bib25); [Cen et al., 2024](#bib.bib12)), zero-sum polymatrix
games ([Leonardos et al., 2021](#bib.bib47)) and potential games ([Cen
et al., 2022a](#bib.bib48)). Under bandit/preference feedback,
value-biased bandit-based methods have been proposed that, like DPO
([Rafailov et al., 2023](#bib.bib34)), exploit the closed-form optimal
policy to bypass the two-step RLHF procedure, for both offline ([Cen et
al., 2025](#bib.bib31)) and online settings ([Cen et al.,
2025](#bib.bib31); [Xie et al., 2025](#bib.bib32); [Zhang et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib33)). These results were further extended to
game-theoretic settings ([Wang et al., 2023](#bib.bib35); [Ye et al.,
2024](#bib.bib17)). [Yang et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) develop
value-incentivized algorithms for learning NE in zero-sum matrix games
and Coarse Correlated Equilibrium (CCE) in general-sum Markov games.
However, none of these approaches leverage the structure of KL
regularization and maintain a $`\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})`$ regret. More
recently [Zhao et al. (2025a)](#bib.bib39) achieved
$`\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon)`$ sample complexity in the KL-regularized
contextual bandits setting with a strong coverage assumption on the
reference policy. Subsequently, [Zhao et al. (2025b)](#bib.bib1);
[Tiapkin et al. (2024)](#bib.bib10) proposed optimistic bonus–based
algorithms for KL-regularized bandits and RL that achieve logarithmic
regret ($`\mathcal{O}(\beta^{-1}d^{2}\log^{2}(T))`$ in bandits and
$`\mathcal{O}(\beta^{-1}H^{5}d^{3}\log^{2}(T))`$ in RL)²² 2 For
uniformity, we report the sample complexities under linear function
approximation/linear MDP and per-step rewards $`r_{h}\in[0,1]`$ and
trajectory reward $`\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h}\in[0,H]`$. without coverage
assumptions, leveraging the closed-form optimal policy in their
analysis. However, their results are limited to the single-player
setting, where the optimal policy admits a closed-form expression in
terms of the reward model. Similar faster convergence guarantees were
also achieved for the RL setting by [Foster et al. (2025)](#bib.bib36)
and for offline contextual bandits with $`f`$-divergences ([Zhao et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib37)).

##### Game-theoretic Methods in LLM Alignment.

Fine-tuning LLMs with RL is a core part of modern post-training
pipelines, enhancing reasoning and problem-solving ([Guo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib50)). Game-theoretic and self-play methods extend RL to
multi-agent settings, with applications in alignment ([Calandriello et
al., 2024](#bib.bib65); [Rosset et al., 2024](#bib.bib53); [Munos et
al., 2024](#bib.bib67); [Zhang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib66)) and reasoning
([Cheng et al., 2024](#bib.bib51); [Liu et al., 2025](#bib.bib56)).
Within this paradigm, self-play optimization is framed as an online
two-player matrix/Markov game, where models iteratively improve using
their own responses by solving for the NE ([Wu et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib52); [Chen et al., 2024](#bib.bib54); [Swamy et al.,
2024](#bib.bib55); [Tang et al., 2025](#bib.bib60); [Wang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib68)). More broadly, game theory has been applied to
modeling non-transitive preferences ([Swamy et al., 2024](#bib.bib55);
[Ye et al., 2024](#bib.bib17); [Tiapkin et al., 2025](#bib.bib71)),
enabling collaborative post-training and decision-making ([Park et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib58); [Park et al., 2025b](#bib.bib57)), accelerating
Best-of-N distillation ([Yang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib59)), and for
multi-turn alignment/RLHF ([Wu et al., 2025a](#bib.bib28); [Shani et
al., 2024](#bib.bib61)), among other LLM applications.

### 1.3 Paper organization and Notation

The remainder of the paper is structured as follows. Section
[2](#S2 "2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
presents the algorithm and results on Matrix Games, while Section
[3](#S3 "3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
extends them to Markov Games. We provide concluding remarks in Section
[5](#S5 "5 Conclusion ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
and outline the proofs in Section
[4](#S4 "4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games").
Complete proofs are deferred to the appendix.

Notation: For $`n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}`$, we use $`[n]`$ to denote the index
set $`\{1,\cdots,n\}`$. We use $`\Delta^{n}`$ to denote the
$`n`$-dimensional simplex, i.e.,
$`\Delta^{n}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x\geq 0,\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=1\}.`$
The Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between two distributions $`P`$ and
$`Q`$ is denoted by
$`\mathrm{KL}(P\,\|\,Q):=\sum_{x}P(x)\log\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}.`$ For a
matrix $`M\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}`$, we denote by $`M(i,:)`$ its
$`i`$-th row and by $`M(:,j)`$ its $`j`$-th column. We use
$`\mathcal{O}(\cdot)`$ to denote the standard order-wise notation and
$`\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)`$ is used to denote order-wise notation
which suppresses any logarithmic dependencies.

## 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games

### 2.1 Problem Setup

We first consider two-player zero-sum matrix games as the foundation of
our algorithmic framework. The KL-regularized payoff function is given
as

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
f^{\mu,\nu}(A)=\mu^{\top}A\nu-\beta\text{KL}(\mu\|\mu_{\text{ref}})+\beta\text{KL}(\nu\|\nu_{\text{ref}}),
``` |  | (1) |

where $`\mu\in\Delta^{m}`$ (resp. $`\nu\in\Delta^{n}`$) denotes the
policy of the max (resp. min) player. The reference policy
$`\mu_{\text{ref}}\in\Delta^{m}`$ (resp.
$`\nu_{\text{ref}}\in\Delta^{n}`$) encodes prior strategies for the max
(resp. min) player and is used to incorporate prior knowledge about the
game (e.g., pretrained policies). Here, $`A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}`$
is the true (unknown) payoff matrix and $`\beta\geq 0`$ is the
regularization parameter. The Nash Equilibrium (NE)
$`(\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star})`$ is defined as the solution of the
following saddle-point problem.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mu^{\star}=\arg\max_{\mu\in\Delta^{m}}\min_{\nu\in\Delta^{n}}f^{\mu,\nu}(A)\quad\text{and}\quad\nu^{\star}=\arg\min_{\nu\in\Delta^{n}}\max_{\mu\in\Delta^{m}}f^{\mu,\nu}(A).
``` |  | (2) |

For the NE policies $`(\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star})`$ and all
$`\mu\in\Delta^{m}`$, $`\nu\in\Delta^{n}`$ we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle f^{\mu,\nu^{\star}}\leq f^{\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star}}\leq f^{\mu^{\star},\nu}.`$ |  | (3) |

Noisy Bandit Feedback. The matrix $`A`$ is unknown and can be accessed
through noisy oracle bandit queries. For any $`i\in[m]`$ and
$`j\in[n]`$, we can query the oracle and receive feedback
$`\widehat{A}(i,j)`$ where

|     |                              |     |
|-----|------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \widehat{A}(i,j)=A(i,j)+\xi.
       ```                           |     |

Here, $`\xi`$ is i.i.d zero mean subgaussian random variable with
parameter $`\sigma>0`$. We are interested in learning the NE of the
matrix game
([1](#S2.E1 "In 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
in a sample-efficient manner using as few queries as possible.

##### Goal: Regret Minimization.

We define the dual-gap corresponding to the policy pair $`(\mu,\nu)`$ as

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\quad\textsf{DualGap}(\mu,\nu):=f^{{\star},\nu}(A)-f^{\mu,{\star}}(A),
``` |  |

where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle f^{{\star},\nu}(A):=\max_{\mu\in\Delta^{m}}f^{\mu,\nu}(A)\qquad\text{and}\qquad f^{\mu,{\star}}(A):=\min_{\nu\in\Delta^{n}}f^{\mu,\nu}(A).`$ |  | (4) |

The dual gap can be viewed as the total exploitability ([Davis et al.,
2014](#bib.bib79)) of the policy pair $`(\mu,\nu)`$ by the respective
opponent.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\quad\textsf{DualGap}(\mu,\nu)=\underbrace{f^{{\star},\nu}(A)-f^{\mu,\nu}(A)}_{\text{Exploitability of the min player policy }\nu}+\underbrace{f^{\mu,\nu}(A)-f^{\mu,{\star}}(A)}_{\text{Exploitability of the max player policy }\mu}.
``` |  |

The dual gap of the Nash equilibrium policy pair
$`(\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star})`$ is zero (see
([3](#S2.E3 "In 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))).
In order to capture the cumulative regret of both the players over T
rounds, for a sequence of policy pairs
$`\left\{\left(\mu_{t},\nu_{t}\right)\right\}_{t=1}^{T}`$, the
cumulative regret over $`T`$ rounds is given by the sum of dual gaps

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{Regret}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textsf{DualGap}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\mu_{t},{\star}}(A)\right).
``` |  |

1: Input: Reg. parameter $`\beta`$, regularization, iteration number
$`T`$, ref. policies $`(\mu_{\text{ref}},\nu_{\text{ref}})`$.

2: Initialization: Dataset $`\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\emptyset`$,
$`\lambda>0`$, initial parameter $`\omega_{0}`$

3: for $`t=1,\cdots,T`$ do

4:   Compute the LMSE matrix
$`\overline{A}_{t}:=A_{\overline{\omega}_{t}}`$ where

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\overline{\omega}_{t}=\arg\min_{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{(i,j,\widehat{A}(i,j))\in\mathcal{D}_{t-1}}\left(A_{\omega}(i,j)-\widehat{A}(i,j)\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}
``` |  | (5) |

5:   Compute the Nash equilibrium $`(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})`$ of the matrix
game with the current parameter $`\overline{A}_{t}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mu_{t}=\arg\max_{\mu\in\Delta^{m}}\min_{\nu\in\Delta^{n}}f^{\mu,\nu}(\overline{A}_{t}),\quad\nu_{t}=\arg\min_{\nu\in\Delta^{n}}\max_{\mu\in\Delta^{m}}f^{\mu,\nu}(\overline{A}_{t}).
``` |  | (6) |

6:   Compute optimistic matrix games for both players using $`b_{t}`$ in
([9](#S2.E9 "In Bonus Function. ‣ 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A^{+}_{t}:=\overline{A}_{t}+b_{t}\quad A^{-}_{t}:=\overline{A}_{t}-b_{t}
``` |  | (7) |

7:   Compute best response pairs under optimism

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tilde{\mu}_{t}=\arg\max_{\mu\in\Delta^{m}}f^{\mu,\nu_{t}}(A^{+}_{t}),\quad\tilde{\nu}_{t}=\arg\min_{\nu\in\Delta^{n}}f^{\mu_{t},\nu}(A^{-}_{t}).
``` |  | (8) |

8:   Data collection: Sample
$`(i_{t}^{+},j_{t}^{+})\sim(\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t})`$,
$`(i_{t}^{-},j_{t}^{-})\sim(\mu_{t},\tilde{\nu}_{t})`$ and collect noisy
feedback $`\widehat{A}(i^{+}_{t},j^{+}_{t})`$ and
$`\widehat{A}(i^{-}_{t},j^{-}_{t})`$. Update the dataset
$`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{+}=\mathcal{D}_{t-1}^{+}\cup\left\{(i^{+}_{t},j^{+}_{t},\widehat{A}(i^{+}_{t},j^{+}_{t}))\right\}`$
and
$`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-}=\mathcal{D}_{t-1}^{-}\cup\left\{(i^{-}_{t},j^{-}_{t},\widehat{A}(i^{-}_{t},j^{-}_{t}))\right\}`$.
$`\mathcal{D}_{t}=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{+}\cup\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-}`$.

9: end for

Algorithm 1 Optimistic Matrix Game ($`\mathrm{OMG}`$)

### 2.2 Algorithm Development

We propose a model-based algorithm (Algorithm
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
called Optimistic Matrix Game ($`\mathrm{OMG}`$) based on UCB-style
bonuses ([Auer et al., 2002](#bib.bib80)). To enable function
approximation, we parameterize the payoff matrix by $`A_{\omega}`$ with
$`\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ as the parameter vector. At each step
$`t\in[T]`$, $`\mathrm{OMG}`$ estimates the payoff matrix based on
collected samples and collects bandit feedbacks using the optimistic
best response policy pairs. To elaborate further,

- •
  Payoff matrix update: Given the set $`\mathcal{D}_{t-1}`$, the matrix
  $`\overline{A}_{t}`$ is computed as the model that minimizes the
  regularized least-squares loss between the model and the collected
  feedback
  ([5](#S2.E5 "In 4 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
  The policy pair $`(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})`$ is computed as the
  KL-regularized NE policies under the payoff matrix
  $`\overline{A}_{t}`$.
- •
  Data collection using optimistic best-response pairs: The optimistic
  model $`A_{t}^{+}`$ (resp. $`A_{t}^{-}`$) for the max (resp. min)
  players is computed by adding (resp. subtracting) the bonus matrix
  $`b_{t}`$ to the MSE matrix $`\overline{A}_{t}`$
  ([7](#S2.E7 "In 6 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
  Each player’s best response under its respective optimistic model is
  obtained by fixing the other’s strategy
  ([8](#S2.E8 "In 7 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")),
  yielding policy pairs $`(\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t})`$ and
  $`(\mu_{t},\tilde{\nu}_{t})`$. We sample
  $`(i_{t}^{+},j_{t}^{+})\sim(\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t})`$,
  $`(i_{t}^{-},j_{t}^{-})\sim(\mu_{t},\tilde{\nu}_{t})`$ and collect
  noisy feedback $`\widehat{A}(i^{+}_{t},j^{+}_{t})`$ and
  $`\widehat{A}(i^{-}_{t},j^{-}_{t})`$.

### 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees

###### Assumption 1 (Linear function approximation ([Yang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib7))).

The true payoff matrix belongs to the function class

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
A_{\omega}(i,j):=\langle\omega,\phi(i,j)\rangle,\quad\forall i\in[m],j\in[n],
``` |  |

where $`\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ is the parameter vector, and
$`\phi(i,j)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ is the feature vector associated with the
$`(i,j)^{\text{th}}`$ entry. The feature vectors are known and fixed,
satisfying $`\|\phi(i,j)\|_{2}\leq 1`$ $`\forall\;i\in[m],`$
$`j\in[n]`$.

###### Assumption 2 (Realizability).

There exists $`\omega^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ such that
$`A=A_{\omega^{\star}}`$ and $`\|\omega^{\star}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}`$.

##### Bonus Function.

Under Assumption
[1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (Linear function approximation ( , )). ‣ 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
given $`\delta\in(0,1)`$, the bonus matrix $`b_{t}`$ at time $`t`$ is
defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle b_{t}(i,j)=\eta_{T}\|\phi(i,j)\|_{\Sigma_{t}^{-1}},`$ |  | (9) |

wherein
$`\Sigma_{t}=\lambda\mathbf{I}+\sum_{(i,j)\in{\mathcal{D}_{t-1}}}\phi(i,j)\phi(i,j)^{\top}`$
and
$`\eta_{T}=\sigma\sqrt{d\log\left(\frac{3(1+2T/\lambda)}{\delta}\right)}+\sqrt{\lambda d}`$.

##### Regret Guarantees.

We now present the main results for the $`\mathrm{OMG}`$ algorithm.
Theorem
[2.1](#S2.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.1. ‣ Regret Guarantees. ‣ 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
establishes a logarithmic regret bound that depends on the
regularization strength $`(\beta)`$ alongside a
$`\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{T})`$ regularization independent bound. Full
proofs are deferred to Appendix
[B](#A2 "Appendix B Matrix Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games").

###### Theorem 2.1.

Under Assumptions
[1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1 (Linear function approximation ( , )). ‣ 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
and
[2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2 (Realizability). ‣ 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
for any fixed $`\delta\in(0,1)`$ and reference policies
$`(\mu_{\text{ref}},\nu_{\text{ref}})`$, choosing $`\lambda=1`$ and
$`b_{t}(i,j)`$ per eq.
([9](#S2.E9 "In Bonus Function. ‣ 2.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
in Algorithm
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
we have the following guarantees hold simultaneously w.p. $`1-\delta`$

- •
  Regularization-dependent guarantee: For any $`\beta>0`$, we have

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\forall\;T\in\mathbb{N}^{+}:\qquad\mathrm{Regret}(T)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\beta^{-1}d^{2}\left(1+\sigma^{2}\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{d}\right)\right).`$ |  |
- •
  Regularization-independent guarantee: For any $`\beta\geq 0`$, we have

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\forall\;T\in\mathbb{N}^{+}:\qquad\mathrm{Regret}(T)\leq\mathcal{O}\left((1+\sigma)d\sqrt{T}\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right).`$ |  |

Under bounded noise $`\sigma`$, $`\mathrm{OMG}`$ achieves a regret bound
of

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min\{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T}),\,\mathcal{O}(\beta^{-1}d^{2}\log^{2}(T/\delta))\},
``` |  |

which grows only logarithmically with $`T`$. This significantly improves
upon the prior rate $`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})`$ in [Yang et
al. (2025a)](#bib.bib7) under KL-regularization. For smaller values of
$`T`$ or the regularization parameter $`\beta`$ (even $`\beta=0`$),
$`\mathrm{OMG}`$ recovers the $`\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})`$
regret guarantee of the standard algorithms designed for the
unregularized setting through the regularization-independent bound.
Consequently, $`\mathrm{OMG}`$ can learn an $`\varepsilon`$-NE using

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min\{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}),\,\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\beta^{-1}d^{2}/\varepsilon)\}
``` |  |

samples.

## 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games

In this section, we will now extend our ideas to two-player zero-sum
Markov games.

### 3.1 Problem Setup

We consider a two-player zero-sum KL-regularized Markov game with a
finite horizon represented as
$`\mathcal{M}:=\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},P,r,H\}`$ where
$`\mathcal{S}`$ is a possibly infinite state space,
$`\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V}`$ are the finite action spaces of the max and
min players respectively. $`H\in\mathbb{N}^{+}`$ is the horizon and
$`P=\{P_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}`$ where
$`P:\mathcal{S\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\Delta(S)`$
is the set of inhomogeneous transition kernels and
$`r=\{r_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}`$ with
$`r_{h}:\mathcal{S\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow[0,1]`$
the reward function. Here, we will focus on the class of Markovian
policies $`\mu:=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}`$ (resp.
$`\nu:=\{\nu_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}`$) for the max (resp. min) player, where
the action of each player at any step $`h`$ only depends on the current
state ($`\mu_{h}:\mathcal{S\times}[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{U})`$
and $`\nu_{h}:\mathcal{S\times}[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{V})`$) with
no dependence on the history. For reference policies
$`\mu_{\text{ref}}:\mathcal{S\times}[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{U})`$,
$`\nu_{\text{ref}}:\mathcal{S\times}[H]\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{V})`$,
the
$`\forall(s,i,j)\in\mathcal{S\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}},h\in[H]`$
the KL-regularized value and Q-function under this setup is given as
([Cen et al., 2024](#bib.bib12))

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\mu,\nu}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=h}^{H}r_{k}(s_{k},i,j)-\beta\log\frac{\mu_{k}(i|s_{k})}{\mu_{\text{ref},k}(i|s_{k})}+\beta\log\frac{\nu_{k}(j|s_{k})}{\nu_{\text{ref},k}(j|s_{k})}\middle|s_{h}=s\right],`$ |  | (10) |
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\mu,\nu}(s,i,j)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=r_{h}(s,i,j)+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{\prime}\sim P_{h}(\cdot|s_{h},i,j)\end{subarray}}\left[V_{h+1}^{\mu,\nu}(s^{\prime})\right].`$ |  | (11) |

The value function can be expressed in terms of the Q function as
follows.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\mu,\nu}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\;\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\sim\mu_{h}(\cdot\mid s)\\[2.0pt]
j\sim\nu_{h}(\cdot\mid s)\end{subarray}}\!\left[Q_{h}^{\mu,\nu}(s,i,j)-\beta\log\frac{\mu_{h}(i|s)}{\mu_{\text{ref},h}(i|s)}+\beta\log\frac{\nu_{h}(j|s)}{\nu_{\text{ref},h}(j|s)}\right]`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\sim\mu_{h}(\cdot\mid s),\\[2.0pt]
j\sim\nu_{h}(\cdot\mid s)\end{subarray}}\!\left[Q_{h}^{\mu,\nu}(s,i,j)\right]-\beta\text{KL}\left(\mu_{h}(\cdot|s)\|\mu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)\right)+\beta\text{KL}\left(\nu_{h}(\cdot|s)\|\nu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)\right).`$ |  | (12) |

For fixed policy $`\nu`$ of the min player, the best response value
function of the max player is defined as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall s\in\mathcal{S},h\in[H]:\quad V_{h}^{{\star},\nu}(s)=\max_{\mu}V_{h}^{\mu,\nu}(s).`$ |  | (13) |

The associated policy is called the best response policy and is denoted
as $`\mu^{\dagger}(\nu)`$. If $`\mu^{\dagger}`$ is the best response of
the max player to a fixed strategy $`\nu`$ of the min player, then
solving eq.
([13](#S3.E13 "In 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
we get

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall i\in\mathcal{U},s\in\mathcal{S},h\in[H]\qquad\mu^{\dagger}_{h}(i|s)=\frac{\mu_{\text{ref},h}(i|s)\exp\left(\mathbb{E}_{j\sim\nu_{h}(\cdot|s)}[Q^{\mu^{\dagger},\nu}(s,i,j)/\beta]\right)}{\sum_{i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}}\mu_{\text{ref},h}(i^{\prime}|s)\exp\left(\mathbb{E}_{j\sim\nu_{h}(\cdot|s)}[Q^{\mu^{\dagger},\nu}(s,i^{\prime},j)/\beta]\right)}.`$ |  | (14) |

Similarly, if $`\nu^{\dagger}(\mu)`$ is the best response of the min
player to a fixed strategy $`\mu`$ of the max player we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\forall j\in\mathcal{V},s\in\mathcal{S},h\in[H]\qquad\nu^{\dagger}_{h}(j|s)=\frac{\nu_{\text{ref},h}(j|s)\exp\left(-\mathbb{E}_{i\sim\mu_{h}(\cdot|s)}[Q^{\mu,\nu^{\dagger}}(s,i,j)/\beta]\right)}{\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}}\nu_{\text{ref},h}(j^{\prime}|s)\exp\left(-\mathbb{E}_{i\sim\mu_{h}(\cdot|s)}[Q^{\mu,\nu^{\dagger}}(s,i,j^{\prime})/\beta]\right)}.`$ |  | (15) |

A policy pair $`(\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star})`$ is called the Nash
equilibrium of the Markov game if both the policies $`\mu^{\star}`$ and
$`\nu^{\star}`$ are best responses to each other. The dual gap
associated with a policy pair $`(\mu,\nu)`$ is given by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\textsf{DualGap}(\mu,\nu):=V_{1}^{{\star},\nu}(\rho)-V_{1}^{\mu,{\star}}(\rho).`$ |  |

Here
$`V_{1}^{\mu,\nu}(\rho)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{s_{1}\sim\rho}[V_{1}^{\mu,\nu}(s_{1})]`$
where $`\rho`$ is the initial state distribution. The cumulative regret
associated with sequence of policies
$`\left\{(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})\right\}_{t=1}^{T}`$ is given by the sum of
dual gaps

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Regret}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\textsf{DualGap}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}V_{1}^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(\rho)-V_{1}^{\mu_{t},{\star}}(\rho).`$ |  |

1: Input: Reg. parameter $`\beta`$ iteration no. $`T`$, ref. policies
$`(\mu_{\text{ref}},\nu_{\text{ref}})`$.

2: Initialization: Dataset $`\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\emptyset`$,
$`\lambda\geq 0`$, initial parameters
$`\{\overline{\theta}_{h,0},\theta_{h,0}^{+},\theta_{h,0}^{-}\}_{h=1}^{H}`$.

3: for $`t=1,\cdots,T`$ do

4:   for $`h=H,H-1\cdots,1`$ do

5:    Regress onto MSE Bellman target, optimistic Bellman targets for
each player

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overline{\theta}_{h,t}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\sum_{k=1}^{|\mathcal{D}_{t-1}|}\left(f_{h}^{\theta}(s_{h,k},i_{h,k},j_{h,k})-r_{h,k}-\overline{V}_{h+1,t}(s_{h+1,k})\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\theta\|^{2}_{2},`$ |  | (16a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{h,t}^{+}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\sum_{k=1}^{|\mathcal{D}_{t-1}|}\left(f_{h}^{\theta}(s_{h,k},i_{h,k},j_{h,k})-r_{h,k}-V_{h+1,t}^{+}(s_{h+1,k})\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\theta\|^{2}_{2},`$ |  | (16b) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{h,t}^{-}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\sum_{k=1}^{|\mathcal{D}_{t-1}|}\left(f_{h}^{\theta}(s_{h,k},i_{h,k},j_{h,k})-r_{h,k}-V_{h+1,t}^{-}(s_{h+1,k})\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\theta\|^{2}_{2}.`$ |  | (16c) |

6:    Compute MSE, superoptimistic Q functions for both players

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overline{Q}_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\Pi_{h}\left\{f^{\overline{\theta}_{h,t}}_{h}(s,i,j)\right\},`$ |  | (17a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h,t}^{+}(s,i,j)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\Pi_{h}^{+}\left\{f_{h}^{\theta_{h,t}^{+}}(s,i,j)+b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)\right\},`$ |  | (17b) |
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h,t}^{-}(s,i,j)`$ | $`\displaystyle:=\Pi_{h}^{-}\left\{f_{h}^{\theta_{h,t}^{-}}(s,i,j)-b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)\right\}.`$ |  | (17c) |

7:    Compute Nash equilibrium w.r.t. LMSE game

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle(\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s),\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s))\leftarrow\text{Nash Zero-sum}_{\beta}((\overline{Q}_{h,t})(s,\cdot,\cdot)).`$ |  | (18) |

8:    Compute Optimistic Best Responses (BR) for both players

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\text{BR}_{\beta}(Q_{h,t}^{+}(s,\cdot,\cdot),\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)),\quad\tilde{\nu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\leftarrow\text{BR}_{\beta}(Q_{h,t}^{-}(s,\cdot,\cdot),\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)).`$ |  | (19) |

9:    Compute the value functions

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overline{V}_{h,t}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\sim\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\\
j\sim\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\end{subarray}}\left[\overline{Q}_{h,t}(s,i,j)\right]-\beta\text{KL}(\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\|\mu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s))+\beta\text{KL}(\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\|\nu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)),`$ |  | (20a) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\ V_{h,t}^{+}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\sim\tilde{\mu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\\
j\sim\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\end{subarray}}\left[Q_{h,t}^{+}(s,i,j)\right]-\beta\text{KL}(\tilde{\mu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\|\mu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s))+\beta\text{KL}(\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\|\nu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)),`$ |  | (20b) |
|  | $`\displaystyle V_{h,t}^{-}(s)`$ | $`\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\sim\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\\
j\sim\tilde{\nu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\end{subarray}}\left[Q_{h,t}^{-}(s,i,j)\right]-\beta\text{KL}(\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\|\mu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s))+\beta\text{KL}(\tilde{\nu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)\|\nu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)).`$ |  | (20c) |

10:   end for

11:   Data Collection: Receive initial state $`s_{1,t}\sim\rho`$,
execute the policies $`(\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t})`$ and
$`(\mu_{t},\tilde{\nu}_{t})`$ to sample trajectories
$`\tau^{+}_{t}=\left\{(s_{h,t}^{+},i_{h,t}^{+},j_{h,t}^{+},r_{h,t}^{+},s_{h+1,t}^{+})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}`$
and
$`\tau^{-}_{t}=\left\{(s_{h,t}^{-},i_{h,t}^{-},j_{h,t}^{-},r_{h,t}^{-},s_{h+1,t}^{-})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}`$
respectively. Update the dataset
$`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{+}=\mathcal{D}_{t-1}^{+}\cup\left\{\tau_{t}^{+}\right\}`$
and
$`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-}=\mathcal{D}_{t-1}^{-}\cup\left\{\tau_{t}^{-}\right\}`$,
$`\mathcal{D}_{t}=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{+}\cup\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-}`$.

12: end for

Algorithm 2 Super-Optimistic Markov Game ($`\mathrm{SOMG}`$)

### 3.2 Algorithm Development

We propose a model-free algorithm (Algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
called $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ which uses bonuses based on superoptimistic
confidence intervals, larger than the ones used in standard UCB style
analysis ([Auer et al., 2002](#bib.bib80)) to ensure efficient
exploration-exploitation tradeoff and achieve logarithmic regret. To
enable function approximation, we use the function class
$`f_{h}^{\theta}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}`$
parameterized by $`\theta\in\Theta`$ for the regression step
([16](#S3.EGx13 "In 5 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
The $`Q`$ functions are obtained subsequently using a projection
operation
([17](#S3.E17 "In 6 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
The algorithm, on a high level maintains three $`Q`$ and $`V`$
functions, estimates superoptimistic best response for each player by
solving stagewise matrix games and performs data collection using the
best response policy pairs. Here we further elaborate the algorithm:

- •
  Q-function updates: $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ maintains three value
  ($`\overline{V}_{h}`$, $`V^{+}_{h}`$ and $`V^{-}_{h}`$) and $`Q`$
  functions ($`\overline{Q}_{h}`$, $`Q^{+}_{h}`$ and $`Q^{-}_{h}`$). The
  Q functions are updated in two steps. 1) Solving the regularized least
  mean squared error with respective bellman targets ($`r_{h}+V_{h+1}`$)
  using data collected until $`t-1`$ ($`\mathcal{D}_{t-1}`$).
  ([16](#S3.EGx13 "In 5 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
  followed by a 2) projection step
  ([17](#S3.E17 "In 6 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
  wherein the $`Q`$ functions are projected onto respective feasible
  regions. The projection operator is defined as follows

  |  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|----|
  |  |  |  |  |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\Pi_{h}(x)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\max\{0,\min\{x,H-h+1\}\},`$ |  | (21a) |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\Pi^{+}_{h}(x)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\max\left\{0,\min\{x,3(H-h+1)^{2}\}\right\},`$ |  | (21b) |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\Pi^{-}_{h}(x)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\min\left\{-3(H-h+1)^{2},\max\{x,H-h+1\}\right\}.`$ |  | (21c) |

  The projection operator is designed to enable superoptimism by
  choosing a ceiling higher than the maximum attainable value. Standard
  optimistic algorithms use a the same projection operator for the
  optimistic estimates of both the players
  $`\Pi_{h}^{\text{opt}}(x)=\max\{0,\min\{x,(H-h+1)\}\}`$.
- •
  Superoptimism:³³ 3 A similar concept called over-optimism where extra
  padding is added to the bonus was used in single-agent RL ([Agarwal et
  al., 2023](#bib.bib75)) for a different purpose of maintaining
  monotonicity of variance estimates. To calculate the superoptimistic
  $`Q`$ function for the max (resp. min) player we add (resp. subtract)
  the super optimistic bonus ($`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}`$). Standard
  optimism only adds an optimistic bonus $`b_{h,t}`$
  ([22](#S3.EGx19 "In 2nd item ‣ 3.2 Algorithm Development ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
  which is a high probability upper bound on the Bellman error of the
  superoptimistic Q function (called optimistic $`Q`$ function under
  vanilla optimism):

  |  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|----|
  |  |  |  |  |  |
  |  | $`\displaystyle\left|f_{h}^{\theta^{+}_{h,t}}(s,i,j)-r_{h}(s,i,j)+PV_{h+1}^{+}(s,i,j)\right|`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq b_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ |  | (22a) |
  |  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h,t}^{+}(s,i,j)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\Pi\left(f_{h}^{\theta^{+}_{h,t}}(s,i,j)+b_{h,t}(s,i,j)\right).`$ |  | (22b) |

  However $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ uses a superoptimistic bonus defined as:

  |  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)=b_{h,t}(s,i,j)+2b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h,t}(s,i,j),`$ |  | (23) |

  where the additional bonus $`b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ is a high
  probability upper bound on the Bellman error in the MSE $`Q`$ function

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\left|\overline{Q}_{h}(s,i,j)-r_{h}(s,i,j)+P\overline{V}_{h+1}(s,i,j)\right|\leq b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h,t}(s,i,j),`$ |  |

  which results in the super optimistic $`Q`$ function being strictly
  greater than the high confidence upper bound
  ([22](#S3.EGx19 "In 2nd item ‣ 3.2 Algorithm Development ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
  one obtains from optimism.
- •
  Best response computation: The stage-wise NE policy pair
  $`(\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s),\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s))`$ is computed by solving the
  KL regularized zero-sum matrix
  ([2](#S2.E2 "In 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
  game with the payoff matrix being
  $`A=\overline{Q}_{h,t}(s,\cdot,\cdot)`$ and reference policies
  $`\mu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)`$ and $`\nu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|s)`$
  ([18](#S3.E18 "In 7 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
  The policies $`\tilde{\mu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)`$ and
  $`\tilde{\nu}_{h,t}(\cdot|s)`$ are computed as the best responses to
  policies $`\nu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)`$ and $`\mu_{h,t}(\cdot|s)`$ under
  matrix games with payoff matrices $`Q^{+}_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ and
  $`Q_{h,t}^{-}(s,i,j)`$ respectively.
- •
  Value function update and Data collection: The value functions
  $`\overline{V}_{h,t}(s)`$, $`V_{h,t}^{+}(s)`$ and $`V_{h,t}^{-}(s)`$
  are updated via the Bellman equation
  ([12](#S3.E12 "In 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
  using policy pairs $`(\mu_{h,t},\nu_{h,t})`$,
  $`(\tilde{\mu}_{h,t},\nu_{h,t})`$, and
  $`(\mu_{h,t},\tilde{\nu}_{h,t})`$, respectively
  ([20](#S3.EGx17 "In 9 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
  We use $`\text{KL}(a|b)(s)`$ as shorthand for
  $`\text{KL}(a(\cdot|s)|b(\cdot|s))`$. Two new trajectories

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  |
  ``` math
  \tau_{t}^{+}=\left\{(s_{h,t}^{+},i_{h,t}^{+},j_{h,t}^{+},r_{h,t}^{+},s_{h+1,t}^{+})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{t}^{-}=\left\{(s_{h,t}^{-},i_{h,t}^{-},j_{h,t}^{-},r_{h,t}^{-},s_{h+1,t}^{-})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}
  ``` |  |

  are collected by following policies
  $`(\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t})=\left\{(\tilde{\mu}_{h,t},\nu_{h,t})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}`$
  and
  $`(\mu_{t},\tilde{\nu}_{t})=\left\{(\mu_{h,t},\tilde{\nu}_{h,t})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}`$
  respectively. Update the dataset
  $`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{+}=\mathcal{D}_{t-1}^{+}\cup\left\{\tau_{t}^{+}\right\}`$
  and
  $`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-}=\mathcal{D}_{t-1}^{-}\cup\left\{\tau_{t}^{-}\right\}`$,
  $`\mathcal{D}_{t}=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{+}\cup\mathcal{D}_{t}^{-}`$.

##### Computational Benefit of Regularization.

The steps in eq.
([8](#S2.E8 "In 7 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
of Algorithm
[1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Goal: Regret Minimization. ‣ 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
as well as eq.
([18](#S3.E18 "In 7 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
and
([19](#S3.E19 "In 8 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
of Algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
require solving for the NE of a KL-regularized zero-sum matrix game.
This can be accomplished using extragradient methods ([Cen et al.,
2023](#bib.bib25)), which guarantee last-iterate linear convergence. In
contrast, solving the corresponding problem in the unregularized setting
only yields an $`\mathcal{O}(1/T)`$ convergence rate.

### 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees

###### Assumption 3 (Linear MDP [Jin et al. (2020)](#bib.bib4); [Xie et al. (2023)](#bib.bib11)).

The MDP $`\mathcal{M}:=\{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{U},\mathcal{V},r,P,H\}`$
is a linear MDP with features
$`\phi:\mathcal{S\times U\times V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ and for
every $`h\in[H]`$ there exists an unknown signed measure
$`\psi_{h}(\cdot)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ over $`\mathcal{S}`$ and an unknown
fixed vector $`\omega_{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ such that

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P_{h}(\cdot\mid s,i,j)=\langle\phi(s,i,j),\psi_{h}(\cdot)\rangle,\qquad r_{h}(s,i,j)=\langle\phi(s,i,j),\omega_{h}\rangle.
``` |  |

Without loss of generality, we assume $`\|\phi(s,i,j)\|\leq 1`$ for all
$`(s,i,j)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}`$, and
$`\max\{\|\psi_{h}(\mathcal{S})\|,\|\omega_{h}\|\}\leq\sqrt{d}\quad\text{for all }h\in[H]`$.

We use linear function approximation with
$`f_{h}^{\theta}(s,i,j):=\langle\theta,\phi(s,i,j)\rangle`$ and
$`\Theta=\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ under which we get linearity for free since
the following proposition holds:

###### Proposition 3.1.

Under Assumption
[3](#Thmassumption3 "Assumption 3 (Linear MDP ( ) ). ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
for the Nash equilibrium policy
$`(\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star})=(\mu^{\star}_{h},\nu^{\star}_{h})_{h=1}^{H}`$
there exist weights
$`\{\theta^{\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star}}_{h}\}_{h=1}^{H}`$ such that
$`\forall(s,i,j)\in\mathcal{S\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{V}},h\in[H]`$,
we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{h}^{\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star}}(s,i,j)=\left\langle\phi(s,i,j),\theta_{h}^{\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star}}\right\rangle\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\left|Q_{h}^{\mu^{\star},\nu^{\star}}(s,i,j)\right|\in[0,H-h+1].`$ |  |

Proof. The proof of Proposition
[3.1](#S3.Thmproposition1 "Proposition 3.1. ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
is contained in the proofs of Lemma
[C.5](#A3.Thmlemma5 "Lemma C.5. ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
and
[C.8](#A3.Thmlemma8 "Lemma C.8 (Linearity of the Q function). ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games").
 

Note that $`\mathcal{D}_{t-1}`$ contains $`2(t-1)`$ trajectories; for
convenience we index them by $`\tau`$, with each trajectory of the form
$`\left\{(s_{h}^{\tau},i_{h}^{\tau},j_{h}^{\tau},r_{h}^{\tau},s_{h+1}^{\tau})\right\}_{h=1}^{H}`$.
We define $`\Sigma_{h,t}`$ as follows:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Sigma_{h,t}:=\lambda\mathbf{I}+\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{t-1}}\phi(s_{h}^{\tau},i_{h}^{\tau},j_{h}^{\tau})\phi(s_{h}^{\tau},i_{h}^{\tau},j_{h}^{\tau})^{\top}.`$ |  |

The expressions for $`\overline{\theta}_{h,t}`$, $`\theta_{h,t}^{+}`$
and $`\theta_{h,t}^{-}`$ are given by

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\overline{\theta}_{h,t}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\Sigma_{h,t}^{-1}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{t-1}}\phi_{h,\tau}\left[r_{h,\tau}+\overline{V}_{h+1,t}(s^{\tau}_{h+1})\right],`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{h,t}^{+}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\Sigma_{h,t}^{-1}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{t-1}}\phi_{h,\tau}\left[r_{h,\tau}+V^{+}_{h+1,t}(s^{\tau}_{h+1})\right],`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\theta_{h,t}^{-}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\Sigma_{h,t}^{-1}\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{D}_{t-1}}\phi_{h,\tau}\left[r_{h,\tau}+V^{-}_{h+1,t}(s^{\tau}_{h+1})\right],`$ |  |

##### Bonus Function.

Under Assumption
[3](#Thmassumption3 "Assumption 3 (Linear MDP ( ) ). ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
the superoptimistic bonus function $`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}`$ is defined
as follows

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}(s,i,j):=b_{h,t}(s,i,j)+2b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)
``` |  |

with

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)=\eta_{1}\|\phi(s,i,j)\|_{\Sigma_{h,t}^{-1}}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{h,t}(s,i,j)=\eta_{2}\|\phi(s,i,j)\|_{\Sigma_{h,t}^{-1}}.`$ |  | (24) |

We choose
$`\eta_{1}=c_{1}\sqrt{d}H\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{16T}{\delta}\right)}`$
and
$`\eta_{2}=c_{2}dH^{2}\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{16dTH}{\delta\min\{1,\beta\}}\right)}`$
for some determinable universal constants $`c_{1},c_{2}>0`$.

##### Regret Guarantees.

We now present the main results for the $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ algorithm.
Theorem
[3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 3.1. ‣ Regret Guarantees. ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
establishes a logarithmic regret bound that depends linearly on the
inverse of regularization strength $`(\beta^{-1})`$, alongside a
traditional $`\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{T})`$ bound. Full proofs are
deferred to Appendix
[C](#A3 "Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games").

###### Theorem 3.1.

Under assumption
[3](#Thmassumption3 "Assumption 3 (Linear MDP ( ) ). ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
for any reference policies
$`(\mu_{\text{ref}},\nu_{\text{ref}})=(\left\{\mu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|\cdot)\right\}_{h=1}^{H},\left\{\nu_{\text{ref},h}(\cdot|\cdot)\right\}_{h=1}^{H})`$,
any fixed $`\delta\in[0,1]`$, choosing $`\lambda=1`$ and
$`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ as per eq.
([24](#S3.E24 "In Bonus Function. ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
in algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
we have the following guarantees hold simultaneously w.p.
$`(1-\delta)`$: for any $`\beta>0`$, we have

- •
  Linear in $`\beta^{-1}`$ logarithmic regret guarantee:

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\forall\;T\in\mathbb{N}^{+}:\qquad\mathrm{Regret}(T)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\beta^{-1}d^{3}H^{7}\log^{2}\left(\frac{dTH}{\delta\min\{1,\beta\}}\right)\right).`$ |  |
- •
  $`\sqrt{T}`$ regret guarantee:

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | $`\displaystyle\forall\;T\in\mathbb{N}^{+}:\qquad\mathrm{Regret}(T)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(d^{3/2}H^{3}\sqrt{T}\log\left(\frac{dTH}{\delta\min\{1,\beta\}}\right)\right),`$ |  |

As demonstrated in Theorem
[3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 3.1. ‣ Regret Guarantees. ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
for the regularized ($`\beta>0`$) setting, $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$, achieves a
regret bound of

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min\left\{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{3/2}H^{3}\sqrt{T}),\,\mathcal{\widetilde{O}}(\beta^{-1}d^{3}H^{7}\log^{2}(T/\delta))\right\},
``` |  |

which grows only logarithmically⁴⁴ 4 By employing Bernstein-based ([Xie
et al., 2021](#bib.bib76)) bonuses in $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$, one could
potentially shave off an additional $`Hd^{1/2}`$ factor in the
$`\sqrt{T}`$ bound and an $`H^{2}d`$ factor in the logarithmic regret
bound. with $`T`$. Consequently, $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ needs only

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\min\left\{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d^{3}H^{6}/\varepsilon^{2}),\,\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\beta^{-1}d^{3}H^{7}/\varepsilon)\right\}
``` |  |

samples to learn an $`\varepsilon`$-NE.

##### Reduction to the Single-agent Setting.

Both $`\mathrm{OMG}`$ and $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ naturally reduce to
multi-armed Bandit and single-agent RL respectively when the
min-player’s action space is a singleton. Note that, in the single-agent
case, the positive KL term
$`\beta\text{KL}(\nu(\cdot|s)\|\nu_{\text{ref}}(\cdot|s))`$ vanishes for
every state $`s`$, and the value functions in
([10](#S3.E10 "In 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
are bounded above by $`H`$. Since the min-player no longer needs to make
decisions, the steps in
([16c](#S3.E16.3 "In 16 ‣ 5 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")),
([17c](#S3.E17.3 "In 17 ‣ 6 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")),
and
([20c](#S3.E20.3 "In 20 ‣ 9 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
do not affect the dynamics and are no longer required. Consequently, we
can set the ceiling in the projection operator
([21b](#S3.E21.2 "In 21 ‣ 1st item ‣ 3.2 Algorithm Development ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
to be of order $`\mathcal{O}(H)`$ and design the bonus functions
$`b_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ and $`b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ with linear
dependence on $`H`$ (as opposed to quadratic dependence for
$`b_{h,t}(s,i,j)`$ in the game setting). This yields improved regret
guarantees of
$`\mathcal{\widetilde{O}}\left(\beta^{-1}d^{3}H^{5}\log^{2}\bigl(\tfrac{dT}{\delta}\bigr)\right)`$
and $`\mathcal{\widetilde{O}}\left(d^{3/2}H^{2}\sqrt{T}\right)`$, while
all other aspects of the algorithm remain unchanged.

##### Technical Challenges.

We discuss a few technical challenges that are worth highlighting.

- •
  Absence of closed form expressions for NE policies: In single-agent
  settings (bandits and RL), analyses of algorithms achieving
  logarithmic regret rely on the fact that the optimal policy for a
  given transition–reward model pair directly admits a Gibbs-style
  closed-form solution ([Zhao et al., 2025b](#bib.bib1); [Zhao et al.,
  2025a](#bib.bib39); [Tiapkin et al., 2024](#bib.bib10)). In contrast,
  in game-theoretic settings, no such direct closed-form expression
  exists for Nash equilibrium policies. The same absence of closed form
  expressions also arises in Coarse Correlated Equilibrium (CCE)–based
  approaches, which are commonly employed to achieve
  $`\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T})`$ regret when learning Nash equilibrium for
  zero-sum games ([Xie et al., 2023](#bib.bib11); [Jin et al.,
  2022](#bib.bib24); [Chen et al., 2022](#bib.bib9); [Liu et al.,
  2021](#bib.bib23)). We address this challenge by leveraging best
  response sampling, where the best response to a fixed opponent policy
  does admit a closed-form expression.
- •
  Unbounded value functions: Moreover in the single-agent RL setting
  with KL regularization, the value function does not include any
  positive KL regularization terms. Thus, both the value and
  $`Q`$-functions are upper bounded by $`H`$. As a consequence, the
  optimistic $`Q`$-function is bounded within $`[0,H]`$. This
  boundedness enables the direct construction of confidence intervals
  for the optimistic $`Q`$-function using standard concentration
  results, which in turn allows algorithms from the unregularized
  setting to be carried over to the regularized setting with minimal
  modifications. However, in the KL-regularized game
  ([10](#S3.E10 "In 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))([11](#S3.E11 "In 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")),
  the value functions contain positive KL terms, which can cause them to
  take arbitrarily large values exceeding $`H`$. This makes it
  challenging to construct confidence intervals for the optimistic
  (superoptimistic in our case) $`Q`$-functions directly. We solve this
  problem using best response sampling and superoptimism. (More details
  in Section
  [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
- •
  Covering number bounds: Lastly, bounding the covering number of the
  induced superoptimistic value function class is non-trivial due to its
  complicated structure involving the best response to the NE policy of
  a different game. We exploit the properties of smoothness in variation
  of the NE policies in the KL-regularized setting to bound the covering
  number, which results in the $`\log(1/\min\{1,\beta\})`$ term in our
  regret bounds, and this might be of independent interest.

## 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms

### 4.1 Matrix Games

The cumulative regret can be decomposed as the cumulative sum of
exploitability of the min and the max player

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | Regret(T) | $`\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\mu_{t},{\star}}(A)\right)=\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(f^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(A))}_{\text{Exploitability of the max player}}+\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(f^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\mu_{t},{\star}}(A))}_{\text{Exploitability of the min player }}.`$ |  | (25) |

We bound the first term (exploitability of the max player) and the
bounding of the second term follows analogous arguments. Now we have the
following concentration inequality for Matrix games. The first term in
eq.
([25](#S4.E25 "In 4.1 Matrix Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
can be further decomposed as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(f^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(A))}_{\text{Exploitability of the max player}}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(f^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t}}(A))}_{T_{1}}+\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}(f^{\tilde{\mu}_{t},\nu_{t}}(A)-f^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(A))}_{T_{2}}`$ |  |

We will now analyze these terms individually.

##### Bandits view for bounding $`T_{1}`$.

By construction of the algorithm, the strategies $`\mu_{t}`$,
$`\tilde{\mu}_{t}`$, and $`\dot{\mu}_{t}`$ are best responses to the
common fixed strategy $`\nu_{t}`$ of the min-player under the payoff
matrices $`\overline{A}_{t}`$, $`A^{+}_{t}`$, and $`A`$ respectively.
This property not only provides closed-form representations but also
facilitates cancellation of the KL terms corresponding to $`\nu_{t}`$ in
$`T_{1}`$ and $`T_{2}`$. As a result of fixed $`\nu_{t}`$, one can view
the min-player strategy $`\nu_{t}`$ as part of the environment and bound
$`T_{1}`$ the same way as done in bandits with the max player as the
decision making entity.

##### Regularization-dependent Bound.

Traditional regret analysis in matrix games ignores the regularization
terms and bounds the regret using the sum of bonuses
$`c\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[b_{t}(i,j)]`$ which is further bounded as
$`\sqrt{T}\log(T)`$ using Jensen’s inequality and the elliptical
potential lemma/eluder dimension (Lemma
[A.6](#A1.Thmlemma6 "Lemma A.6 (Elliptical Potential Lemma). ‣ Appendix A Useful Lemmas ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
However in the presence of regularization the originally payoff
landscape, linear in $`\mu`$ and $`\nu`$
([1](#S2.E1 "In 2.1 Problem Setup ‣ 2 Two-Player Zero-Sum Matrix Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
becomes $`\beta`$ strongly convex in the policy $`\nu`$ and $`\beta`$
strongly concave in $`\mu`$. Under the full information setting it is
well known that this facilitates design of algorithms that achieve
faster convergence to the equilibrium ([Cen et al., 2023](#bib.bib25);
[Cen et al., 2024](#bib.bib12)). This intuitively suggests one can also
design algorithms which achieve sharper regret guarantees in the
regularized setting under bandit feedback. Specifically we show that we
can bound the regret by the sum of squared bonuses
$`c\beta^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[b_{t}(i,j)^{2}]`$ which enables
using to circumvent the need for Jensen’s inequality which contributes
the $`\sqrt{T}`$ term and directly bound the terms using the elliptical
potential lemma (Lemma
[A.6](#A1.Thmlemma6 "Lemma A.6 (Elliptical Potential Lemma). ‣ Appendix A Useful Lemmas ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
to obtain a $`\mathcal{O}(\beta^{-1}\log^{2}(T))`$ regret. We detail the
analysis as follows.

Leveraging the bandits view, one can bound the term $`T_{1}`$ adapting
the arguments from [Zhao et al. (2025b)](#bib.bib1) (Theorem 4.1) as
Section [B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Proof of Theorem : Regularization-dependent Bound ‣ Appendix B Matrix Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
to obtain
$`T_{1}\leq c\beta^{-1}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\sim\tilde{\mu}_{t}}\left[\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{j\sim\nu_{t}}[(b_{t}(i,j))]\right)^{2}\right]`$.
In order to bound the term $`T_{2}`$ we use a mean value theorem based
argument (detailed in Section
[B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 Proof of Theorem : Regularization-dependent Bound ‣ Appendix B Matrix Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
Step 2) and the property

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle 2(|A^{+}_{t}(i,:)-\overline{A}_{t}(i,:)|\nu_{t})\geq(|A^{+}_{t}(i,:)-A(i,:)|\nu_{t})`$ |  | (26) |

to show that
$`T_{2}\leq c^{\prime}\beta^{-1}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\sim\tilde{\mu}_{t}}\left[\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{j\sim\nu_{t}}[(b_{t}(i,j))]\right)^{2}\right]`$.
Thus we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle T_{1}+T_{2}\leq c^{\prime\prime}\beta^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\sim\tilde{\mu}_{t}}\left[\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{j\sim\nu_{t}}[(b_{t}(i,j))]\right)^{2}\right]\leq c^{\prime\prime}\beta^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\sim\tilde{\mu}_{t}\\
j\sim\nu_{t}\end{subarray}}\left[\left(b_{t}(i,j)\right)^{2}\right].`$ |  |

The final bound is obtained by substituting the expression for the bonus
terms and using Lemmas
[A.2](#A1.Thmlemma2 "Lemma A.2 (Martingale Concentration, Lemma B.2 in [ ] ). ‣ Appendix A Useful Lemmas ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
and
[A.6](#A1.Thmlemma6 "Lemma A.6 (Elliptical Potential Lemma). ‣ Appendix A Useful Lemmas ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
and using analogous arguments to bound the second term in eq.
([25](#S4.E25 "In 4.1 Matrix Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
resulting in

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Regret}(T)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\beta^{-1}d^{2}\left(1+\sigma^{2}\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{d}\right)\right).`$ |  |

##### Regularization-independent Bound.

The term $`T_{1}`$ can be bounded by
$`\mathcal{O}\left((1+\sigma)d\sqrt{T}\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right)`$
using similar arguments to ones used in standard UCB bounds as done in
Section
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Proof of Theorem : Regularization-independent Bound ‣ Appendix B Matrix Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
step 1. We bound $`T_{2}`$ by
$`\mathcal{O}\left((1+\sigma)d\sqrt{T}\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right)`$
as detailed in Section
[B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Proof of Theorem : Regularization-independent Bound ‣ Appendix B Matrix Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
step 2. Similarly bounding the second term in eq.
([25](#S4.E25 "In 4.1 Matrix Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{Regret}(T)\leq\mathcal{O}\left((1+\sigma)d\sqrt{T}\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right).`$ |  |

### 4.2 Markov Games

In this section we extend the arguments from the matrix games section to
design and analyse the $`\mathrm{SOMG}`$ Algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
for achieving logarithmic regret in Markov games. We begin by
elaborating some algorithmic choices before proceeding with the proof
outline. The value function in eq.
([10](#S3.E10 "In 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
which can be rewritten as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle V_{h}^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(s):=`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}\left[\sum_{k=h}^{H}r_{k}(s_{k},i,j)-\beta\text{KL}\left(\mu_{k}(\cdot|s_{k})\|\mu_{\text{ref},k}(\cdot|s_{k})\right)+\beta\text{KL}\left(\nu_{k}(\cdot|s_{k})\|\nu_{\text{ref},k}(\cdot|s_{k})\right)\middle|s_{h}=s\right].`$ |  |

This can be unbounded from both above and below depending on $`\mu_{t}`$
and $`\nu_{t}`$ due to the unbounded nature of the KL regularization
terms. For instance, if $`\nu_{t}`$ deviates substantially from the
reference policy $`\nu_{\text{ref}}`$ in certain states, the max-player
can exploit this by selecting policies that steer the MDP toward those
states, thereby attaining a higher overall return in regions where the
KL divergence between $`\nu_{t}`$ and $`\nu_{\text{ref}}`$ is large.
This unbounded nature of the value function is problematic when
designing confidence intervals for bellman errors. We address this
problem by choosing the policy pair $`(\mu_{h,t},\nu_{h,t})`$ to the
Nash equilibrium policies under the matrix game $`\overline{Q}_{h,t}`$
in eq.
([18](#S3.E18 "In 7 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
As a consequence of this choice we have for any $`\beta>0`$ (full
details in Lemma
[C.6](#A3.Thmlemma6 "Lemma C.6. ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\beta\text{KL}\left(\mu_{h,t}(.|s_{h})\|\mu_{\text{ref},h}(.|s_{h})\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle\in[0,H-h+1],`$ |  | (27) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\beta\text{KL}\left(\nu_{h,t}(.|s_{h})\|\nu_{\text{ref},h}(.|s_{h})\right)`$ | $`\displaystyle\in[0,H-h+1].`$ |  | (28) |

From eq.
[28](#S4.E28 "In 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
one can show for the policies $`(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})`$ Algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
chooses, we have
$`V_{h}^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(s)\in[-c_{1}(H-h+1)^{2},c_{2}(H-h+1)^{2}]`$.
(Lemma
[C.7](#A3.Thmlemma7 "Lemma C.7 (Range of Q, V functions). ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
and one can proceed to bound Bellman errors for the resulting policies.
This is also the reason our projection operator
([21](#S3.E21 "In 1st item ‣ 3.2 Algorithm Development ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
has the ceiling of the order $`(H-h+1)^{2}`$ as opposed to standard
$`(H-h+1)`$ as done in most unregularized works [Xie et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib11). The constant 3 comes from superoptimism (lemma
[C.4](#A3.Thmlemma4 "Lemma C.4 (Super-optimistic gap). ‣ C.1 Supporting Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).

We also use properties of optimism and superoptimistic gap in our
proofs. For notational simplicity, while stating the these properties we
will omit the superscript $`\nu_{t}`$ and also the dependence on $`t`$.
The properties hold for all $`t\in[T]`$. Consequently, the symbol
$`\mu`$ here should be interpreted as the time-indexed policy
$`\mu_{t}`$, rather than an arbitrary policy.

##### Optimism.

For the setting in algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
and any policy $`\mu^{\prime}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ | $`\displaystyle\geq\overline{Q}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})\quad\text{and}\quad Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})\geq Q_{h}^{\mu^{\prime}}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h}).`$ |  | (29) |

##### Superoptimistic gap.

For the setting in algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle 2\left|\left(Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})-\overline{Q}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})\right)\right|\geq\left|Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})-Q_{h}^{\mu}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})\right|.`$ |  | (30) |

Standard analysis that achieves $`\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{T})`$ regret
uses just optimism meaning they just need
$`Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})\geq Q_{h}^{{\dagger}}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$
and thus they only add the bonus term $`b_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ to
account for the bellman error incurred while regression used to compute
$`Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ (since the bellman error of the term
$`Q_{h}^{{\dagger}}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ is 0). However for our proof
technique we additionally require the property in eq.
([30](#S4.E30 "In Superoptimistic gap. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
to hold. Under optimism property in eq.
([29](#S4.E29 "In Optimism. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
the eq.
([30](#S4.E30 "In Superoptimistic gap. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
is equivalent to

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left(Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})-\overline{Q}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})\right)\geq\overline{Q}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})-Q_{h}^{\mu}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h}).`$ |  | (31) |

This property follows as a consequence of the design of the
superoptimistic bonus
([24](#S3.E24 "In Bonus Function. ‣ 3.3 Theoretical Guarantees ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
and projection operator
([21](#S3.E21 "In 1st item ‣ 3.2 Algorithm Development ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
As detailed in Lemma
[C.4](#A3.Thmlemma4 "Lemma C.4 (Super-optimistic gap). ‣ C.1 Supporting Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"),
we enable this by the addition of the bonus
$`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})=b_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})+2b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$
where $`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ adjusts for the
Bellman error in the term $`Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ while
$`2b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ adjusts for the bellman
errors in the the two $`\overline{Q}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ terms
while the Bellman error of the term $`Q_{h}^{\mu}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$
is 0 in
([31](#S4.E31 "In Superoptimistic gap. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
The property holds with just plain optimism when $`H=1`$ for matrix
games.

Lastly note that the bonus is superoptimistic in the sense that we add
the term $`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ while constructing
$`Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ in eq.
([17b](#S3.E17.2 "In 17 ‣ 6 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
although we have with high probability the highest value (optimistic
value) of $`Q^{+}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ can be upperbounded just by
adding $`b_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ - the standard optimistic bonus yet
we add
$`b^{\texttt{sup}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})=b_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})+2b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$
where $`b^{\texttt{mse}}_{h}(s_{h},i_{h},j_{h})`$ is the bonus used in
addition to optimism.

##### Design of the superoptimistic projection operator.

Recall that the projection operator in eq.
([21b](#S3.E21.2 "In 21 ‣ 1st item ‣ 3.2 Algorithm Development ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
is given by

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\Pi^{+}_{h}(x)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\max\left\{0,\min\{x,3(H-h+1)^{2}\}\right\}.`$ |  |

We can show (Lemma
[C.7](#A3.Thmlemma7 "Lemma C.7 (Range of Q, V functions). ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
that the maximum value that can be attained by any policy’s
($`\mu^{\prime}`$) value function

|     |                                                     |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       Q_{h}^{\mu^{\prime},\nu_{t}}(s,i,j)\leq(H-h+1)^{2}.
       ```                                                  |     |

However, during the projection operation we set the projection ceiling
to $`3(H-h+1)^{2}`$. This is again done to facilitate the
superoptimistic gap in eq.
([30](#S4.E30 "In Superoptimistic gap. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
when the $`Q^{+}_{h}(s,i,j)`$ attains its ceiling value.

The dual gap at time $`t`$ can be decomposed as follows

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\text{{DualGap}}(\mu_{t},\nu_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=V_{1}^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(s_{1})-V_{1}^{\mu_{t},{\star}}(s_{1})=\underbrace{V_{1}^{{\star},\nu_{t}}(s_{1})-V_{1}^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(s_{1})}_{\text{Exploitability of the max player}}+\underbrace{V_{1}^{\mu_{t},\nu_{t}}(s_{1})-V_{1}^{\mu_{t},{\star}}(s_{1})}_{\text{Explotaibility of the min player}}.`$ |  | (32) |

We elaborate the bounding of the first term (exploitability of the max
player) and the bounding of the second term follows analogous arguments.
One can further decompose the first term in eq.
([32](#S4.E32 "In Design of the superoptimistic projection operator. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
V_{1}^{{\star},\nu}(s_{1})-V_{1}^{\mu,\nu}(s_{1})=\underbrace{V_{1}^{{\star},\nu}(s_{1})-V_{1}^{\tilde{\mu},\nu}(s_{1})}_{T_{5}}+\underbrace{V_{1}^{\tilde{\mu},\nu}(s_{1})-V_{1}^{\mu,\nu}(s_{1})}_{T_{6}}.
``` |  | (33) |

##### RL view for bounding $`T_{5}`$.

As a result of fixed $`\nu_{t}`$, one can view the min-player strategy
$`\nu_{t}`$ as part of the environment and bound $`T_{5}`$ the same way
as done in RL with the max player as the decision making entity. Here
$`\mu^{\dagger}_{h}`$ and $`\tilde{\mu}_{h}`$ are stagewise best
responses to the fixed strategy $`\nu_{h}`$ under matrix games with
parameters $`Q_{h}^{\mu^{\dagger},\nu}`$ and $`Q_{h}^{+}`$ respectively

##### Regret guarantees.

Leveraging the RL view one can bound the term $`T_{5}`$ adapting the
arguments from [Zhao et al. (2025b)](#bib.bib1) (Theorem 5.1) and
accounting for changing $`\nu_{t}`$ as detailed in section
[C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Proof of Theorem : Logarithmic Regret Bound ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
step 1 for the logarithmic regret bound and standard single agent RL
analysis as detailed in
[C.3.1](#A3.SS3.SSS1 "C.3.1 𝛽>0: Regularized setting ‣ C.3 Proof of Theorem : Traditional √𝑇 bound ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
step 1 for the traditional $`\sqrt{T}`$ bound. This does not require
anything beyond the standard optimism property
([29](#S4.E29 "In Optimism. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
The bounding of $`T_{6}`$ is elaborated in section
[C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Proof of Theorem : Logarithmic Regret Bound ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
step 2 for the logarithmic regret bound and section
[C.3.1](#A3.SS3.SSS1 "C.3.1 𝛽>0: Regularized setting ‣ C.3 Proof of Theorem : Traditional √𝑇 bound ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
step 2 for the $`\sqrt{T}`$ and requires both optimism
([29](#S4.E29 "In Optimism. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
and superoptimistic gap
([30](#S4.E30 "In Superoptimistic gap. ‣ 4.2 Markov Games ‣ 4 Proof Overview and Mechanisms ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
properties.

##### Bounding the covering number of the superoptimistic value function class.

The superoptimistic value function class is induced by the $`Q`$
functions in Algorithm
[2](#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")
Eq.
([20](#S3.EGx17 "In 9 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ 3.1 Problem Setup ‣ 3 Two-Player Zero-Sum Markov Games ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
The covering number of MSE value functions $`\overline{V}`$ is easier to
bound (Lemma
[C.10](#A3.Thmlemma10 "Lemma C.10 (Covering number of the MSE Value function class in Algorithm ). ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
and relies on the smoothness of the value of the game under payoff
matrix perturbations arguments. Meanwhile, the superoptimistic value
functions $`V^{+}`$ ($`V^{-}`$ can be bounded using symmetric arguments)
is harder to bound (Lemma
[C.11](#A3.Thmlemma11 "Lemma C.11 (Covering number of the superoptimistic value function class in Algorithm ). ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games"))
as it is the value function corresponding to the best response under
$`Q^{+}`$ to the min player’s NE policy under $`\overline{Q}`$. We
exploit the smoothness property of the KL-regularized setting to obtain
this covering number and subsequently use an appropriate cover on the
$`Q^{+}`$ and $`\overline{Q}`$ function classes to get the covering
number of the superoptimistic value function class (Lemma
[C.11](#A3.Thmlemma11 "Lemma C.11 (Covering number of the superoptimistic value function class in Algorithm ). ‣ C.5 Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ Appendix C Markov Game Proofs ‣ Achieving Logarithmic Regret in KL-Regularized Zero-Sum Markov Games")).
Moreover, since the $`Q`$ functions use different projection operators
for different $`h\in[H]`$, we have to split the $`\delta`$ budget across
$`h\in[H]`$.

## 5 Conclusion

In this work, we develop algorithms that achieve provably superior
sample efficiency in competitive games under KL regularization. For
matrix games, we introduced $`\mathrm{OMG}`$, based on optimistic
best-response sampling, and for Markov games, we developed
$`\mathrm{SOMG}`$, which relies on super-optimistic best-response
sampling. Both methods attain regret that scales only logarithmically
with the number of episodes $`T`$. Our analysis leverages the fact that
in two-player zero-sum games, best responses to fixed opponent
strategies admit closed-form solutions. To our knowledge, this is the
first work to characterize the statistical efficiency gains under KL
regularization in game-theoretic settings.

Several avenues for future work remain open, including deriving
instance/gap-dependent regret guarantees under KL regularization that
also capture the dependence on reference policies and developing offline
counterparts of optimistic best-response sampling that achieve superior
sample efficiency with KL regularization under reasonable coverage
assumptions. Extending our methods to general multi-agent settings,
where the objective is to compute CCE and best responses or optimal
policies do not admit a closed-form expression is another promising
direction.

## Appendix

## Appendix A Useful Lemmas

###### Lemma A.1 (Covering number of the $`\ell_{2}`$ ball, Lemma D.5 in [Jin et al. \[2020\]](#bib.bib4)).

For any $`\epsilon>0`$ and $`d\in\mathbb{N}^{+}`$, the
$`\epsilon`$-covering number of the $`\ell_{2}`$ ball of radius $`R`$ in
$`\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ is at most
$`\left(1+\frac{2R}{\epsilon}\right)^{d}`$.

###### Lemma A.2 (Martingale Concentration, Lemma B.2 in [Foster et al. \[2021\]](#bib.bib6)).

Let $`(X_{t})_{t\leq T}`$ be a sequence of real-valued random variables
adapted to a filtration $`\mathcal{F}_{t}`$ and
$`\mathbb{E}_{t}[\cdot]:=\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{t}]`$ denote the
conditional expectation. Suppose that $`|X_{t}|\leq R`$ almost surely
for all $`t`$. Then, with probability at least $`1-\delta`$, the
following inequalities hold:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}`$ | $`\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[X_{t}]+4R\log(2\delta^{-1}),\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{t-1}[X_{t}]\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}+8R\log(2\delta^{-1}).`$ |  |

###### Lemma A.3 (Confidence Ellipsoid: Theorem 2 [Abbasi-Yadkori et al. \[2011\]](#bib.bib5)).

Let $`\xi_{t}`$ be a conditionally $`R`$ sub-gaussian random variable
adapted to the filtration $`\mathcal{F}_{t}`$ and
$`\{X_{t}\}_{t=1}^{\infty}`$, $`\|X_{t}\|\leq L`$ be a
$`\mathcal{F}_{t-1}`$ measurable stochastic process in
$`\mathbb{R}^{d}`$. Define
$`Y_{t}=\langle X_{t},\theta_{\star}\rangle+\xi_{t}`$ where
$`\left\|\theta_{\star}\right\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{S}`$. Let
$`\overline{\theta}_{t}`$ be the solution to the regularized least
squares problem given by

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\overline{\theta}_{t}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R^{d}}}\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}\left(\langle X_{t},\theta\rangle-Y_{t}\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\theta\|_{2}^{2},
``` |  |

then for any $`\delta\in[0,1]`$, for all $`t\geq 0`$, with probability
atleast $`1-\delta`$ we have

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\left\|\overline{\theta}_{t}-\theta_{\star}\right\|_{V_{t}}\leq R\sqrt{d\log\left(\frac{1+tL^{2}/\lambda}{\delta}\right)}+\sqrt{\lambda S}.`$ |  |

###### Lemma A.4 (Lemma 11 in [Abbasi-Yadkori et al. \[2011\]](#bib.bib5)).

Let $`\{\phi_{s}\}_{s\in[T]}`$ be a sequence of vectors with
$`\phi_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}`$ and $`\|\phi_{s}\|\leq L`$. Suppose
$`\Lambda_{0}`$ is a positive definite matrix and define
$`\Lambda_{t}=\Lambda_{0}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\phi_{s}\phi_{s}^{\top}`$. Then
````
